#10615. What Is the Best UI Testing Tool? 8 Tools Compared by the Job They Do (2026)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The ideal UI testing tool is the one that discovers what your users discover first: broken layouts, usability issues that no longer work, and new accessibility issues in all the browsers your users use before a change goes into the repository. In 2026, most frontend teams don't rely on a single tool to do all of that. The best setups involve two layers: a test runner layer that launches the browser and monitors behavior, and a UI testing and review platform that visually checks the outcome and routes changes to the right stakeholders for sign-off.

Even with the right stack, it still relies on what your team needs to catch. Different teams will focus on different points: a QA team trying to automate checkout flows, a design system team defending shared components, and a single developer working on a tight budget will all be somewhere different. This guide is categorized by the job these UI testing tools do, then connects them.

Most "best UI testing tool" lists rank test runners and visual testing platforms against each other as if they were interchangeable. They aren't. UI testing covers four distinct jobs:

Functional testing: Does the UI respond correctly when a user clicks, types, or hovers?

Visual testing: Does the UI still look right across layout, color, typography, and spacing?

Accessibility testing: Can people using keyboards and screen readers use it, and did this change introduce new violations?

Cross-browser and device coverage: Does it work in Safari on mobile, not just Chrome on a developer's laptop?

There are also other levels of UI tests. Unit tests test the logic but don't interact with the UI. End-to-end (E2E) tests represent the full user journey in a browser but are difficult to set up, hard to troubleshoot when the bug is a visual detail, and can't practically test every state. Between the extremes of unit tests and E2E tests, component tests simulate each component's behavior in a real browser using fixed inputs, offering speed closer to unit tests and user-level coverage closer to E2E. Many teams maintain a thin layer of E2E tests for critical journeys, and they have component tests for the dozens of states a component may be in.

These tools are organized by job so you can stack them rather than choose one winner.

Prices are taken from each vendor's site or published comparisons as of October 2026. Check current pricing before you buy.

These platforms sit on top of your test runner. They capture rendered UI, compare it to approved baselines, and give your team a place to review what changed.

Chromatic is a UI testing and review platform made by the Storybook team. It catches visual, interaction, and accessibility bugs in components and pages before they merge, and it gives developers, designers, and product managers one place to review UI changes and sign off. Chromatic's team is the core maintainer of Storybook, which has about 90,000 GitHub stars and 17 million weekly npm downloads, and a Vitest core maintainer on the team built Chromatic's Vitest integration. Chromatic is used by 45 Fortune 100 companies, including GitHub, Shopify, Microsoft, Adobe, the BBC, and monday.com

Pricing: Free plan with 5,000 snapshots per month (Chrome only), unlimited projects and users. Starter is $179/month for 35,000 snapshots and adds Safari, Firefox, and Edge. Pro is $399/month for 85,000 snapshots. Enterprise is custom, with SSO, SCIM, SLA support, and data retention. TurboSnap snapshots count as 0.2 of a regular snapshot.

Trade-offs: Chromatic is strongest when your UI is built from components. Teams with no component tests can start from Playwright or Cypress E2E tests, but they'll get the most value once components are covered too. Snapshot usage also grows with each browser and viewport you add, so plan coverage before you scale it.

Best for: Design system teams, product teams that want designers and PMs to approve UI changes, React Native teams, and Vitest-first teams that want visual testing without baselines stored in the repo.

Percy is BrowserStack's visual testing platform. It captures screenshots from your existing tests, renders them across browsers and responsive widths, and highlights pixel differences for review.

Strengths: Framework-agnostic SDKs for Playwright, Cypress, Selenium, and Storybook. App Percy covers native mobile apps. The free plan includes 5,000 screenshots per month with unlimited users and projects.

Trade-offs: Percy is page-oriented, and each page rendered in each browser at each width counts as a separate screenshot, so usage climbs quickly with cross-browser coverage. Percy compares feature branches against a base branch, which teams with complex branching should test early. Reviewers see rendered screenshots rather than an inspectable copy of the code under test, so debugging a diff usually means reproducing it locally.

Pricing: Free tier, then paid plans with fixed screenshot allowances and overage.

Best for: Teams already paying for BrowserStack that want visual checks on page-level E2E tests.

Applitools Eyes uses Visual AI to compare screenshots the way a person would, with match levels that can ignore content changes and focus on layout. Its Ultrafast Grid re-renders captured page states across many browser, viewport, and device combinations.

Strengths: Mature AI matching, broad browser and device coverage, Figma and Storybook testing, accessibility testing, and an MCP server. Higher tiers add AI-driven functional testing and plain-English test authoring.

Trade-offs: Applitools is priced and packaged for enterprises. There's a free trial but no free plan, and the Starter plan is $667/month billed annually for 100,000 component checkpoints or 1,000 page checkpoints. Professional and Enterprise are custom-priced.

Best for: Large QA organizations with big cross-browser matrices and budget for a dedicated visual AI platform.

Argos is an open source visual testing platform with SDKs for Playwright, Storybook, Vitest, and Cypress. It adds cloud baselines and team review on top of the screenshots your tests already produce.

Strengths: Simple flat pricing. The Hobby plan includes 5,000 screenshots per month, and Pro is $100/month for 35,000 screenshots, with a budget cap available.

Trade-offs: Argos focuses on visual diffs. Argos compares screenshots captured by your own test runs, so browser coverage depends on what your CI runs, and it doesn't offer hosted mobile simulators. It also has a smaller ecosystem than the larger platforms.

Best for: Small teams that want hosted visual review at a predictable price.

Test runners click, type, and assert. They're free and open source, and every platform above builds on them. On their own, they offer limited visual testing: built-in screenshot assertions store baselines in your repository, rendering can drift between laptops and CI, and designers have no review interface.

Playwright is Microsoft's open-source browser automation framework. It runs tests across Chromium, Firefox, and WebKit, with auto-waiting, trace viewer debugging and code generation, and supports JavaScript, TypeScript, Python, Java, and .NET.

Visual testing: Playwright's toHaveScreenshot assertion compares screenshots against baselines stored in Git. It works, but baselines must be regenerated per operating system, and accepting a change means rerunning with an update flag.

Best for: Teams automating critical user journeys such as sign-up, checkout and login. Pair it with a platform like Chromatic for visual review across real browsers.

Cypress is an open-source testing tool that runs inside the browser, with time-travel debugging that shows the app at each step of a test. It supports both E2E and component testing.

Visual testing: Cypress has no built-in visual comparison and relies on plugins or a platform such as Chromatic, Percy or Applitools. Cypress Cloud, its paid service, adds recording, parallelization, flake detection and add-ons for accessibility and UI coverage.

Best for: Frontend developers who want fast feedback and an interactive test runner while they build.

Storybook is the open source workshop for building UI components in isolation. Each story captures one component state, and those stories double as test cases.

Testing features: Interaction tests with play functions and Testing Library, accessibility checks with the a11y addon, and a Vitest integration that runs stories as component tests.

Visual testing: Storybook doesn't diff screenshots on its own. Chromatic, built by the same team, is its visual testing and review layer.

Best for: Design system and component library teams, and any team practicing component-driven development.

Vitest Browser Mode runs component tests in a real browser instead of a simulated DOM, using Playwright or WebdriverIO under the hood.

Visual testing: Vitest's toMatchScreenshot assertion stores screenshots in the repository. For large projects, this can bloat the repo and baselines drift between machines. Chromatic can take Vitest Browser Mode tests with no code changes and run them in a stable cloud environment instead.

Best for: Teams already writing Vitest tests who want them to check how the UI looks without adopting a new framework.

Start from the problem your team is trying to fix:

"Shared component changes keep breaking other teams' screens." Test at the component level with Storybook or Vitest Browser Mode and Chromatic, so the change shows up on the pull request that caused it.

"Our Playwright tests pass, but the layout still breaks in Safari." Functional assertions don't check appearance. Add a visual testing platform that renders in Safari, such as Chromatic, Percy or Applitools.

"We turned off screenshot tests because of false alarms." Look for deterministic component states and flake controls such as Chromatic's SteadySnap, or AI matching such as Applitools.

"Designers find UI bugs after release." Choose a platform with a review and approval workflow, and make sign-off a required pull request check.

"AI coding agents keep inventing components." Give agents access to your real component library, for example through Chromatic's hosted MCP, and run every agent-built change through the same UI tests as human code.

"We have no budget." Use Playwright or Vitest screenshot assertions, or start on the free tiers of Chromatic, Percy or Argos.

A practical, well-covered setup for a React web app looks like this:

Unit tests in Vitest for logic and utilities.

Storybook stories or Vitest Browser Mode tests for every meaningful component state, with interaction tests on key components.

Chromatic running visual, interaction and accessibility tests on those components in Chrome, Firefox, Safari and Edge on every pull request, with designers assigned as reviewers.

A thin layer of Playwright E2E tests for sign-up, checkout and other critical journeys, also captured in Chromatic so page-level visual changes go through the same review.

This keeps most UI coverage in fast, deterministic component tests, while E2E tests confirm the journeys that matter most still work end to end.

Playwright is the best free tool for E2E UI testing, and Storybook is the best free tool for testing component states. For visual testing, Chromatic, Percy and Argos all offer free tiers of around 5,000 snapshots or screenshots per month.

UI testing is the broad practice of checking that an interface works for users, including functional, visual and accessibility checks. Visual testing is one part of it: comparing rendered UI against approved baselines to catch unintended changes in layout, color, typography and spacing.

No. Chromatic was built by the Storybook team, but it also runs visual tests from Vitest Browser Mode, Playwright and Cypress tests, and from React Native Storybook on iOS and Android simulators.

Playwright's toHaveScreenshot covers basic visual checks, but baselines live in your repo, rendering can differ between machines, and there's no review interface for designers. Teams with several reviewers or many browsers usually add a platform on top.

Most teams use both. Component tests cover individual UI states quickly and reliably, while a smaller set of E2E tests confirms that critical user journeys work across the whole app.

#10613. NASA just used satellites and debris to navigate without GPS

NASA's Starling mission has reached another milestone in autonomous spaceflight by demonstrating a system that can determine a satellite's orbital position using other objects in space as reference points rather than depending on an external navigation network.

The technology, called FALCON (Fast Autonomous Lost-in-space Catalog-based Optical Navigation), is designed to help spacecraft operate with greater independence. Such capabilities could become increasingly important as NASA pursues missions farther from Earth, including lunar satellite swarms, distributed science missions, and future human exploration.

Satellites near Earth commonly rely on GPS for navigation, but those signals may be weak, unreliable, or unavailable around the Moon and in deep space. FALCON offers an alternative by allowing spacecraft to determine where they are using what they can observe around them.

The FALCON payload is a joint flight experiment developed by NASA and EraDrive, a startup that emerged from Stanford University. The system combines EraDrive's Era-Core flight software and embedded algorithms with Starling's cameras and an onboard catalog of known satellites. Together, these tools enable GPS-independent navigation while also helping the spacecraft track activity and objects in its surroundings.

"FALCON is yet another success for the Starling demonstration mission. The results from FALCON can have far-reaching implications for on-orbit space-traffic monitoring, collision avoidance, and alternative navigation," said Roger Hunter, program manager for NASA's Small Spacecraft and Distributed Systems program at NASA's Ames Research Center in California's Silicon Valley. "The number of 'firsts' from Starling just keeps growing."

Using Satellites and Debris as Reference Points

The FALCON demonstration evaluated two related capabilities using Starling's onboard star tracker cameras. These cameras are standard spacecraft instruments that detect bright objects in space and help determine a spacecraft's orientation and position.

During position, navigation, and timing experiments, FALCON identified objects seen by Starling's cameras, including other spacecraft and orbital debris, and compared them with a publicly available catalog of known space objects maintained by the U.S. Department of War. Once the observed objects were identified and verified, FALCON used them as reference points to calculate Starling's orbit.

A separate set of experiments tested whether the spacecraft could improve orbital estimates for the objects it observed. Mission controllers loaded a catalog containing approximately 20,000 space objects and their predicted orbits onto Starling.

FALCON then compared the catalog information with observations gathered by Starling's cameras. Using those measurements, it calculated Starling's position while also refining the estimated locations of other objects in space. The resulting estimates were even more precise than the existing catalog data.

Over a period of three days, FALCON improved the known orbits of more than 200 objects without any intervention from operators on the ground.

A First for Autonomous Optical Navigation

FALCON's ability to determine its own orbit represents a first for a spacecraft navigating with optical cameras based on its position relative to other objects in space. In separate catalog updating tests, Starling also generated better onboard predictions of object positions than those supplied by ground stations.

These abilities could be especially valuable for future networks of satellites operating without GPS. Coordinated satellite systems may play an important role in supporting human activities on the surfaces of the Moon or Mars.

Precise spacecraft positioning is also essential for distributed science missions, where measurements collected from several locations in space must be accurately aligned. For space traffic management, autonomous navigation and onboard catalog updates could reduce dependence on ground-based tracking networks while improving collision avoidance.

From University Research to Commercial Technology

The FALCON experiment also demonstrates NASA's role in helping university research develop into commercial technology. The work began as a University SmallSat Technology Partnerships project before developing into EraDrive, which is now commercializing its Era-Core software and related hardware for wider use.

Starling gave the technology an opportunity to be tested under real conditions in orbit, demonstrating how advanced flight software can allow satellites to function as increasingly autonomous navigators.

Later this year, Starling, which launched in 2023, will expand the FALCON experiment using Era-Core. The mission's four spacecraft will share tracking information with one another and use those combined observations to refine their positions collectively.

NASA's Ames Research Center in California's Silicon Valley leads the Starling mission. NASA's Small Spacecraft and Distributed Systems program, based at Ames and within the Research and Technology Mission Directorate, funds and manages the Starling mission.

#10609. Revisiting Ecstatica - the pioneering survival horror that used its fancy ellipsoid graphics tech to fill its world with dongs

There's a well-known phenomena in video games development known as time-to-penis. It's a somewhat tongue-in-cheek measurement of the time players, when given an exciting suite of new customisation tools, will take to create the first inevitable in-game dong. Ecstatica sort of implies it works the other way round too. That developers - when giddy with a new tech toy - can't always help themselves either. But maybe I'm getting a little ahead of myself.

Ecstatica, developed by Andrew Spencer Studios and published by the long-defunct Psygnosis, originally released back in 1994. It follows a bumbling traveller who unwittingly becomes embroiled in the fate of a demon-cursed medieval village after making a rest stop in search of water. It was heavily inspired by survival horror game granddaddy Alone in the Dark (and arrived a full year before Resident Evil), but while it adopted a similar fixed-camera approach to its adventure, it had some tricks all of its own.

Most immediately notable was the decision to build its pre-rendered backdrops and characters out of ellipsoids; giving the medieval village of Tirih and its surrounding environs a softer, more organic feel compared to the angular lines afforded by the limited polygons of the time. And even today its bold, vibrant, and decidedly spherical aesthetic is quite unlike anything else.

Ecstatica isn't, I should say, new to me. I first played it back on its original release. And it's a game that never quite left my mind; in large part, I'll admit, because of the relative proliferation of dick, boob, and bulge. Faced with the infinite potential ellipsoids could offer, Andrew Spencer Studios' urge to penis was perhaps inevitable, and, lo, the moment our tights-wearing hero dismounts his horses in the opening cinematic, neither his bulbous front bauble or two–giant-eggs-in-a-hanky posterior leave anything to the imagination. And if you opt for the alternative lady hero, well, she's got ample going on there too.

And so it continues: prancing fawns with mega-breasts, muscle gimps with proud chipolatas. Ecstatica really is a singular sort of horror game. But while the cheerfully perky genitalia is undoubtedly what my teenage brain chose to squirrel away in the memory vaults, I also had distant recollections of a game that was intriguing for other, more interesting reasons. So when SNEG - a publisher founded by GOG veterans and focused on reviving lost classics - announced it was re-releasing Ecstatica and its sequel in time for Halloween, I knew I had to go back.

And you know what? After spending a few happy hours revisiting Ecstatica these past few nights, I kind of feel bad I'd mostly condensed it down to its naughty bits (for starters, the pickles, plums, and melons aren't quite as frequent as I remember them). This really is a fascinating game, with a wonderfully, uniquely odd mood - playing something like a bawdy fairytale as it blends daft slapstick silliness with an effectively sinister undertone. This is a game where our hero will stop for an unprovoked pee in a bush or, after being turned into a frog, must seek a kiss from the busty woman who lives down the road. The surprisingly great voice acting, while scant, is pure British whimsy, but throughout the village: demonic destruction, gory evisceration, and crucifixion. And Ecstatica walks a tonal tightrope that somehow works.

It still looks fantastic in its own strange way too; its broad-daylight horrors are vibrantly hued, everything is exuberantly animated, and there's so much characterful incidental detail. As you roam from one jauntily angled screen to the next, searching for objects you'll need to progress - a bible dropped by a hanged priest you can read aloud at the monastery gates, or a flying broomstick you'll need to navigate a boobytrapped dungeon - you'll encounter ale-swigging bears, perpetually annoyed imps with tiny pitchforks, and peeved minotaurs. You'll trip on rocks and inelegantly face-plant; you'll smack your bonce on low ceilings; you'll excitedly put on a suit of armour only to discover it slows you to a comically useless crawl. And you can even kick a priest off the belltower if you're feeling particularly mean.

It's not your usual sort of survival horror, then, but it's got one hell of a trick up its sleeve: recurring enemies that'll readily give chase, notably including an unkillable werewolf that's essentially a precursor to the stalkers - think Resident Evil's Mr. X - beloved by modern-day horror games. You'll catch him peeking around corners as the camera switches to a new angle, he'll launch a surprise attack from the rooftops, or suddenly give chase if you disturb his murdering. And when that happens, he's relentless - the only way you'll lose him is to inflict enough damage he'll give momentary pause, or hide in a cupboard until he moves on. There's even a little stealth. It's rudimentary stuff by today's standards, not helped by the unwieldy combat and tank controls, but it still manages to muster a whiff of tension. And it's remarkable to rediscover something so pioneering in a title I'd mentally reduced to 'the willy game' - particularly when horror's better-known 3D stalker series, like Clock Tower, were yet to come.

Granted, Ecstatica has enough old-school jank that it's not an easy recommendation for modern players; from its wayward combat to its untelegraphed (though pretty amusing) sudden deaths. Or the inventory system, which demands you drop anything your two hands can't hold right there on the floor - so you better remember where you left it. And you're screwed if you accidentally place something in tall grass or just outside the fixed camera frame. SNEG's remaster, though, is decent, and an enjoyable way to experience this cult-classic curio.

And 'curio' is perhaps the best word to describe Ecstatica, with its pioneering survival horror spirit, its ellipsoids, and its unashamed love of slapstick, boobs, and dong. It's been an unexpected pleasure revisiting it some 30 years later, and I'm excited I'll soon have my very first brush with Ecstatica 2. All I know about the 1997 sequel is its hero isn't blessed with a prominent bauble from the off, so its time-to-penis is already looking like a longer one.

#10608. IBM quantum computer solves classically intractable problem in 15 minutes

Scientists used a new error correction strategy to encode 70 logical qubits and tackle a problem considered classically intractable.

The quantum computation was completed in about 15 minutes, while leading classical computing methods would require an impractical amount of time to perform the same task.

IBM and researchers at the University of Chicago have announced a quantum computing demonstration that meets key criteria for quantum advantage. The experiment performed a calculation beyond the practical reach of leading classical simulation techniques while also providing evidence that the quantum computer produced a reliable result.

In a new paper, "Sampling hard circuits with verifiably high fidelity," the researchers describe how they achieved both goals using a newly designed form of encoded quantum circuit. The work represents one of the largest demonstrations of logical quantum computing reported so far.

The circuits and experimental results have also been made publicly available through the Quantum Advantage Tracker.

Why Quantum Results Are So Difficult to Verify

Researchers have long used a benchmark called random circuit sampling (RCS) to explore whether quantum computers can outperform conventional machines.

In simplified terms, RCS challenges a quantum computer to generate patterns that become so complicated that a classical computer cannot efficiently reproduce them. That makes the benchmark useful for testing the limits of classical simulation.

But it also creates a major problem. Once the quantum calculation becomes too difficult for a classical computer to reproduce, verifying that the quantum machine actually produced the correct result becomes increasingly difficult as well. Eventually, checking the answer can itself become infeasible unless researchers make strong assumptions about how the quantum computer behaves internally.

The IBM and University of Chicago team approached this verification problem by developing a more structured alternative to RCS.

The researchers showed that their method preserves the same computational hardness criteria associated with RCS, meaning the problem remains extremely difficult for classical computers. At the same time, the added structure allows errors to be detected during the quantum computation.

"Verification remains one of the biggest challenges in firmly establishing experimental quantum advantage," said Bill Fefferman, Associate Professor at the University of Chicago. "This experiment develops techniques to better characterize the fidelity of hard quantum states under noise, increasing confidence that the quantum computer is solving a computationally hard problem."

Soumik Ghosh, PhD student in Fefferman's group at the University of Chicago, added, "Beyond strengthening experimental validation, advances in verification have the potential to unlock practical applications for the next generation of quantum computers."

The experiment also included one of the world's largest-known demonstrations of quantum error correction.

The researchers operated 70 logical qubits. Unlike individual physical qubits, logical qubits are encoded in a way that helps protect quantum information from errors and noise.

Using those logical qubits, the team carried out 2,415 logical two-qubit operations and 468 logical "T gates," two measures that reflect the complexity of the quantum circuit.

The encoded design significantly improved reliability. Effective logical error rates were 10 times lower than the underlying physical error rates, allowing the circuit to maintain unusually high fidelity even while performing a large number of quantum operations.

IBM Says Quantum Advantage Has Entered a New Stage

"We are now firmly in the quantum advantage era," said Jay Gambetta, Director of IBM Research and IBM Fellow. "We have demonstrated a quantum computation beyond the practical reach of classical computers that establishes, with statistical confidence, a lower bound on how faithfully it was executed. This milestone gives scientists, developers, and businesses a new foundation for trusting quantum computers as they scale to problems far beyond what we can achieve classically."

The researchers found that many leading classical simulation methods would face prohibitive runtimes when attempting the same task.

The IBM quantum computer, by comparison, completed the computation in approximately 15 minutes.

A Step Toward Larger, More Trustworthy Quantum Computers

Speed alone is not enough for quantum computing to become useful at larger scales. Researchers also need ways to suppress errors and establish confidence that a quantum system produced a valid result.

This experiment advances both goals at once by combining large-scale logical quantum computing with a method for evaluating the reliability of a calculation that is already beyond practical classical simulation.

Error correction and trustworthy verification are considered essential for scaling quantum computers toward more difficult problems, making the new demonstration an important step toward that goal.

#10605. Inside Buckshot Roulette developer Mike Klubnika's terrifically nasty horror universe of post-Soviet malaise

In Mike Klubnika's growing oeuvre of brilliantly deplorable games, the same elements tend to recur. First, smoking: deep, long drags to make the lungs burn and eyes water. Second, club music: hard and fast, accented with just a faint sense of melancholy. Third, guns: big and loud, usually aimed at you from point-blank range, ready to blow your brains out.

Across three excellent games in almost as many years - 2023's Buckshot Roulette, 2025's S.p.l.i.t, and 2026's Machine Party - Klubnika has remixed each of these elements with design verve and stylistic aplomb. Buckshot Roulette is a gruesome game of chance delivered at the business end of a pump-action shotgun. S.p.l.i.t. is a Matrix-esque hacking game with the gnarliest, most harrowing ending I've played in recent years. Machine Party, meanwhile, delivers a seedy, hyper-violent take on Mario Party: funny, yes; brutal, too.

But each of these games also contains further tell-tale signs of the expanding Klubnikaverse: grubby low-poly graphics whose every surface seems to be coated in some kind of unpleasant residue; bleak settings that hint at a shared near-future authoritarian setting. These games are short, too, lasting just an hour apiece. But that means they land even harder - thwack - like a pistol whip to the face.

There is a "shared vibe," Klubnika tells me over a video call. "It's like, if you're someone living in this type of universe then you probably don't have what you want most of the time."

Klubnika, a 24-year old university drop-out from the tucked-away enclave of Tallinn, Estonia, describes more specific emotions that he intends his games to arouse: "isolation", "desperation", "risk", "worry." It's hardly crowd-pleasing stuff, but that hasn't stopped his three most recent titles enjoying massive commercial success. Buckshot Roulette has sold 10 million copies. S.p.l.i.t has shifted 200,000. Machine Party currently sits at 1.5 million.

Like other young creatives working in and around horror - notably Backrooms director Kane Parsons and Obsession director Curry Barker - Klubnika was a child of the computer. He was part of a "tech-savvy" friendship group in Tallinn: some made YouTube videos, others were self-taught programmers. Early on, Klubnika made 3D art in Blender while messing about with sound design and video editing. All of them played video games.

Klubnika's desktop PC, then, was both a creative instrument and portal into exotic worlds and taboo-feeling media: Klubnika remembers pirating the edgy top-down shooter Hotline Miami when he was 10 or 11 years old before discovering the music of Aphex Twin in one particularly strange level of the Half-Life multiplayer mod Sven Co-op.

When Klubnika wasn't glued to his screen, he was hitting house parties in Tallinn. But then it was back to the computer with a view to pushing his 3D art skills towards architectural visualisations. However, Klubnika grew bored because "archviz is very technical," he says. "I started making art instead." Klubnika applied to the world-renowned Estonian Academy of Arts, though his application was unsuccessful (clearly, the admissions staff didn't appreciate the young artist's eye for the macabre). Thereafter, he began studying English Language, though he dropped out after a single term because Buckshot Roulette was blowing up.

Ever since, Klubnika seems to have possessed a knack for unlocking Steam's inscrutable algorithm - of winning the wishlist war and riding high on the recommendations tab. This success did not occur overnight: Klubnika self-published 19 small games prior to this moment, five of which are collected on 2023's Unsorted Horror. "I got into game development with the idea it's probably not going to work out as a career," he says. "I did it for the love of the game."

All games are born from specific circumstances: a forest that naturally invites a game of hide and seek; the military-funded MIT computer labs that birthed nascent video games in the 60s. Klubnika's games are no different: first and foremost, their greyscale atmospheres are inspired by the huge, concrete buildings of Tallinn's Soviet era (which offer a stark counterpoint to the city's more straightforwardly picturesque medieval architecture). More specifically, Klubnika, like all young men in Estonia, was required to complete mandatory military service.

He trained in communications, which meant he spent a lot of time fiddling with radios. "I had a pretty easy time," he says, comparing the more technical requirements of his role to the physicality demanded of a machine gunner. "But it was still very demanding," he stresses. Especially challenging was spending large amounts of time away from home: he was stationed three hours away from Tallinn and needed a permit to return.

"There's something a little bit weird about this type of bureaucracy which doesn't feel human to me," says Klubnika. Rather than freely cavorting about Tallinn, he found himself incorporated into a stringently controlled military system. "My games take those ideas to the extreme," he says. "The people in them are very small small cogs in the machine."

It's easy to imagine the protagonist of S.p.l.i.t. toiling away in front of a large, old-fashioned computer as something of a mirror to the radio-operating Klubnika during his military service. But the events of S.p.l.i.t. are no mere training exercise. One mistake and everyone dies, including the player. The game's ending, which involves a devilish triptych of life-ending options - a noose, power drill, and screwdriver - makes this point in awful, hilarious fashion, like a cross between Pi and Eraserhead. It took my breath away and also left me feeling queasy - a testament to Klubnika's commitment to stacking one grim punchline on top of another.

But the Estonian game maker's military service days also yielded moments of levity. He enjoyed smoking with his fellow trainees; a "great ritual," he says, and a means of "decompression" (it's also a pastime Klubnika loves seeing in other media - video games like Arctic Eggs and TV shows such as True Detective). That explains one of his major motifs, then.

Klubnika's eyes also light up at the mention of firearms in the military. "Yeah, we had firearms and shooting drills and stuff," he says, casually noting the "super cool" feeling of firing an AR-15 assault rifle and the "sheer destruction" of a hand grenade. That, then, seems to explain Klubnika's fondness for including ballistic, brain-splattering weaponry in his games. He recalls one explosive training exercise, in particular: "You start off in a bunker and then you go out and you do the exercise. Then you go into a different bunker, and the entire unit goes through. So you're sitting there with a bunch of people that are, like, 20-years-old, and they're all holding high-explosive grenades. It's like, ‘This is crazy bro. Surely nothing will go wrong.'"

The anecdote seems to speak to a central sensibility of Klubnika's gritty games: a foregrounding of violent power that finds no contradiction in the powerlessness of those who wield it. Discussing Machine Party, Klubnika describes the contrapuntal nature of his work in simpler terms. "The idea was to have this fun party game, but it's industrial horror - and it's also kind of sad," he says. At one memorable moment, players get to enjoy respite from the otherwise murderous experience, running across a field of golden wheat, as if fleetingly inhabiting a scene from Terence Malick's 1978 movie, Days of Heaven. Klubnika's right: it is sad - a better life is dangled in front of these people, and then cruelly snatched away.

Barring any kind of major geopolitical rupture involving Estonia, neighbouring Russia, and Europe, Klubnika plans to keep making precisely the type of games that has brought him such success. He has toyed with the idea of scaling up but decided against it for fear of becoming an "administrator" - a suit who delegates the creative process rather than actually does it. "If I end up doing that then a large part of the reason why I make games is gone," he says.

The next game, reveals Klubnika, should harmonise two aspects of his work: the replayable arcade mechanics of Machine Party and Buckshot Roulette, and the single-player story focus of S.p.l.i.t. "I want to find a balance between the two styles," he says.

Regardless of its ultimate form, Klubnika will make it in a staunchly DIY manner: he will laboriously hand-paint each grotty surface, stretch every texture into a warped shadow of itself, and smash together 3D models of varying fidelity into a single scene. In the Klubnikaverse, a nightmarish fun-house distortion of the real world, these kinds of ostensible visual flaws deepen immersiveness rather than interrupt it. This is a fast, dirty way of making video games - and it suits Klubnika's nasty fiction down to its trash-strewn ground.

Nintendo Switch, PC, PS4, PS5, Xbox Series X/S

#10604. Generating Web-Ready Images With AI: Formats, Sizes, and srcset Done Right

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI-generated images are becoming an important part of modern web development. Developers use them for website banners, product visuals, blog illustrations, backgrounds, and other digital content. However, creating an image is only the first step. Preparing it for efficient web delivery is equally important.

Unoptimized images can increase page size, consume unnecessary bandwidth, and delay page rendering. These problems can affect Core Web Vitals, especially Largest Contentful Paint (LCP), which measures how quickly the largest visible content element appears.

This guide explains how to optimize AI-generated images using practical techniques, including image resizing, modern formats, Node.js automation, responsive delivery, and performance testing.

Images often represent a significant portion of a webpage's total download size. When developers upload large images without processing them, visitors may experience slower loading times, particularly on mobile networks.

Large image files that take longer to download.

Incorrect dimensions for different screen sizes.

Missing image dimensions that cause layout shifts.

Excessive compression that reduces visual quality.

Unnecessary downloads caused by poor responsive configuration.

A consistent optimization workflow helps address these problems while maintaining the intended visual appearance.

Before preparing an image, determine where it will appear on the website. A full-width hero banner requires different dimensions from a product thumbnail or a small article illustration.

These are suggested dimensions, not fixed requirements. The actual size depends on the website layout and the devices being supported.

When preparing source visuals, developers can compare different AI image tools to understand how image generation workflows handle aspect ratios, output dimensions, and visual details.

Regardless of the generation method, keep an original high-quality version. This master file can be used to create smaller versions without repeatedly compressing an already optimized image.

Choosing an appropriate image format can reduce file size while maintaining acceptable visual quality.

AVIF: Provides efficient compression for many photographs and detailed illustrations.

WebP: Supports lossy and lossless compression, transparency, and animation.

JPEG: Remains useful for photographs and environments requiring broad compatibility.

PNG: Suitable for screenshots, lossless graphics, and images that require transparency.

No format is automatically the best choice for every image. Developers should compare the resulting file sizes and inspect image quality before selecting a format.

For technical details, consult the MDN Web Docs guide to image file formats.

Manually resizing multiple images can become time-consuming. Automation provides a consistent way to generate different image dimensions and formats.

Sharp is a Node.js library that supports resizing, compression, and image conversion.

The script generates WebP and AVIF versions at several widths.

The quality values are starting points. Adjust them after checking the output for compression artifacts, loss of detail, and unnecessary file size.

For additional configuration options, refer to the official Sharp documentation.

Responsive image delivery allows browsers to select an appropriate image based on available resources and screen layout.

The HTML srcset attribute provides multiple image candidates, while sizes describes the expected rendered width.

The browser uses the available image candidates and layout information to select a suitable resource.

Make sure the sizes attribute accurately reflects the actual website layout. Incorrect values can cause browsers to download larger images than necessary.

Responsive delivery is particularly useful when the same visual appears in different layouts across desktop, tablet, and mobile devices.

The HTML picture element allows developers to provide alternative image formats with a fallback.

The browser selects a supported source, while the img element provides fallback behavior and alternative text.

Always verify that the referenced files exist and that their dimensions match the intended layout.

Images without defined dimensions can cause page content to move while resources are loading. This can negatively affect Cumulative Layout Shift (CLS).

Include width and height attributes whenever possible:

The browser can use the dimensions to reserve the appropriate space before the image finishes loading.

Images below the initial viewport can also use native lazy loading:

However, avoid lazy-loading the main hero image when it contributes to LCP. Important above-the-fold images should be loaded promptly.

Reducing file size should not significantly affect the visual details required by the website.

This is particularly important for images containing:

A practical quality-checking process includes:

Compare the optimized image with its original version.

Inspect the image at its actual display dimensions.

Test the output on mobile and desktop screens.

The smallest file is not necessarily the best option. A slightly larger image may provide noticeably better visual quality.

After implementing the optimization workflow, test the actual website rather than relying only on exported file sizes.

Open Chrome DevTools and select the Network panel. Filter requests by images and reload the webpage.

Mobile devices receive appropriately sized images.

The browser downloads unnecessary image files.

Modern formats are being served correctly.

Important images load without unnecessary delays.

Image dimensions match their display requirements.

You can inspect the image source selected by the browser using:

For websites containing multiple images, inspect each relevant element.

Finally, run an audit using Google PageSpeed Insights. It can help identify oversized images and other performance issues.

The web.dev guide to optimizing LCP also provides useful technical guidance for diagnosing loading delays.

Remember that image optimization is only one part of website performance. Server response times, caching, CSS, and JavaScript execution can also influence page loading.

Uploading original images without checking their dimensions.

Assuming one image format always provides the best compression.

Using incorrect responsive image settings.

Lazy-loading important above-the-fold images.

Ignoring the actual image selected by the browser.

Measuring file size without checking visual quality.

A proper testing process helps identify these issues before they affect visitors.

Four to six sizes may be sufficient for many websites. The ideal number depends on the layout, responsive breakpoints, and supported devices.

No. Lazy loading is useful for images outside the initial viewport. Important images near the top of a webpage should generally load promptly.

The fundamental optimization techniques are the same as those used for conventional images. However, generated visuals may contain complex textures and fine details that require careful inspection after compression.

AI-generated images can improve website design, but efficient delivery requires more than simply exporting and uploading a visual.

Choose suitable dimensions, compare modern image formats, automate processing, and implement responsive delivery. Include image dimensions to prevent layout shifts, prioritize important visual elements, and test the results across different devices.

By following these practices, developers can maintain visual quality, reduce unnecessary downloads, and create faster, more reliable website experiences.

#10603. A “quantum bath” puts quantum entanglement on autopilot

Future quantum computers may depend on the ability to connect widely separated modules through distributed entanglement. Until now, creating this kind of connection has generally required active control and repeated measurements. Physicists at the Institute of Science and Technology Austria (ISTA) have now demonstrated a fully autonomous alternative based on a "quantum bath" made from correlated particles of light.

Published in Physical Review X, the experiment provides the first demonstration of a prediction proposed more than 20 years ago. The approach could eventually offer a new foundation for practical quantum technologies.

Entanglement is one of the defining phenomena of quantum physics. It allows particles or systems to share correlations that cannot be explained by classical physics. Creating distributed entanglement between physically separated qubits (quantum bits) could be important for building larger quantum computers and future quantum networks.

Previous attempts to entangle distant qubits have generally followed one of two strategies. One method sends a single, actively controlled photon from one qubit to another. Another has each qubit emit a photon, with the two photons then matched in an effort to generate entanglement.

The second approach was recognized by the 2022 Nobel Prize in Physics. However, it still depends on repeated measurements and post-selection, and even then, the process does not always successfully produce entanglement.

PhD student Alejandro Andrés-Juanes and professor Johannes Fink at the Institute of Science and Technology Austria (ISTA) worked with international collaborators to develop a different solution. Their system uses a quantum bath that automatically brings distant qubits into synchronization. In a prototype device, the researchers used a shared source of correlated light particles to entangle two separated qubits, experimentally realizing an idea that had remained theoretical for more than two decades.

Quantum entanglement can take several forms. Continuous-variable entangled states can be produced efficiently and are therefore relatively accessible. They can be compared with a pendulum, whose position and momentum change continuously.

Many useful quantum technologies, however, depend on "discrete-variable" systems. These involve 'all-or-nothing' forms of entanglement that stationary qubits can use. The challenge for the ISTA team was finding a way to connect these readily available continuous forms of entanglement with the discrete forms needed for practical applications.

"In this work, we aimed to overcome this mismatch between the readily available and the practically useful forms of entanglement," says Andrés-Juanes. "By stabilizing the entangled states remotely, our approach is fully autonomous and requires no active control or measurement."

A Quantum Bath Powered by Correlated Light

Maintaining both entanglement and quantum coherence is one of the major difficulties facing quantum computing.

The researchers addressed that problem by making the qubits' surrounding environment itself responsible for producing and stabilizing entanglement.

"In our method, the quantum bath -- meaning the qubits' environment -- is the source of entanglement. It creates a new ground state through a continuous stream of correlated photons," says Fink. "This way, the entangled qubit state is stabilized, even beyond the qubits' own 'lifetime', and remains always available as a resource for further quantum processing. This makes the approach conceptually significant."

Because the entangled state remains available, researchers can access it whenever it is needed. That differs from temporary entanglement, which has to be used during the brief period when it exists.

To couple the qubits with the entangled photon source, the researchers relied on microwave photons. These low-energy light particles are especially useful for manipulating quantum information and are already central to leading superconducting-qubit technology.

Optical photons serve a different role and are commonly used in optics and atomic physics. They may also become important for carrying quantum information between distant quantum computers through fiber optics, an area that the Fink group at ISTA is also investigating.

The researchers needed to confirm that the two qubits were actually synchronized inside the quantum bath. To do that, they used quantum tomography, a technique that reconstructs a quantum system by examining many different 'slices' of its behavior.

"Qubits can be in a superposition of states, but all these states collapse when we measure them, leaving us with a 0 or 1 state," says Andrés-Juanes.

Quantum tomography allowed the researchers to perform measurements lasting only 20-80 nanoseconds and use those observations to investigate the qubits' underlying states. A nanosecond is one billionth of a second.

A 20-Year Prediction Becomes an Experiment

By successfully entangling two isolated qubits through a quantum bath, the ISTA researchers created a proof-of-concept laboratory prototype for the long-standing theoretical proposal.

"We present a relatively simple method that could be scaled up to synchronize multiple distant qubits," says Andrés-Juanes.

The new approach is promising, but it is not yet as efficient as methods that actively control qubit states. "Our method currently transfers about 10% of the bath's available entanglement."

The researchers suggest that one reason the idea took more than two decades to demonstrate is that the original theory was developed under idealized conditions that are difficult to reproduce experimentally.

"Our experiments helped us reveal several factors that may have prevented scientists from designing a functional quantum bath using a single source of correlated photons for distributed entanglement," says Fink.

The prototype developed at ISTA could provide new opportunities for quantum-optics experiments. It may also contribute to efforts to expand quantum processors and ultimately move them closer to fault-tolerant operation.

Materials provided by Institute of Science and Technology Austria. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.
